Project 2.8: Production Feature Store & Automated Data Leakage Sentinel

Architect and implement an enterprise feature preprocessing pipeline that automatically inspects tabular schemas, detects target correlation anomalies, isolates temporal boundaries, and halts training when data leakage is detected.

🔗 Full Project Code: part2_classical_ml/ch28_feature_engineering/leak_free_pipeline.py

Case Study Architecture: In large-scale financial platforms (Stripe, Robinhood), data engineers and ML scientists constantly write new feature extraction scripts. If a single junior engineer creates a feature that calculates average user spend over a window that accidentally includes the transaction timestamp, the entire model suffers fatal data leakage. In this project, you construct a continuous feature store sentinel that tests feature-to-target mutual information across time slices and raises automated build alerts on leakage contamination.

Figure 2.9.1: Diagnostic classification evaluation curves. Left: The Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curve plots True Positive Rate versus False Positive Rate across all decision thresholds, with Area Under the Curve (AUC) measuring rank discrimination. Right: The Precision-Recall (PR) curve reliably evaluates severely imbalanced datasets.
Chapter 2.9

Model Evaluation, ROC-AUC & Calibration

The Mann-Whitney U Theorem, Precision-Recall Dynamics & Expected Calibration Error ✦

"When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure."

— Goodhart's Law

Foundation • Core Pillar

1. The Tyranny of Accuracy & The Confusion Matrix Algebra

In toy academic benchmarks with balanced $50/50$ classes, classification accuracy ($\frac{\text{Correct}}{\text{Total}}$) is an intuitive metric. In production enterprise AI, accuracy is virtually useless and actively dangerous.

Consider credit card fraud detection: out of $1,000,000$ transactions, exactly $100$ are fraudulent ($0.01\%$ prevalence). A trivial dummy classifier that outputs return 0 for every transaction achieves a phenomenal $99.99\%$ accuracy while failing to catch a single dollar of fraudulent theft! Evaluating models requires dissecting the Confusion Matrix:

Metric Mathematical Formula Physical Meaning Critical Production Context
Precision (PPV) $\frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FP}}$ When the model predicts Positive, how often is it correct? Spam filters, automated ban hammers (Minimize False Alarms).
Recall (Sensitivity / TPR) $\frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FN}}$ Out of all actual Positive cases, how many did the model catch? Cancer screening, fraud detection (Minimize Missed Detections).
Specificity (TNR) $\frac{\text{TN}}{\text{TN} + \text{FP}}$ Out of all actual Negative cases, how many were correctly identified? Baseline population filtering.
False Positive Rate (FPR) $\frac{\text{FP}}{\text{TN} + \text{FP}} = 1 - \text{Specificity}$ Probability that an actual negative sample is falsely flagged. X-axis of the ROC curve.
$F_1$ Score $2 \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}$ Harmonic mean balancing precision and recall. Penalizes extreme disparities (if Precision=1.0 but Recall=0.01, $F_1 \approx 0.02$).
$F_\beta$ Score $(1 + \beta^2) \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\beta^2 \text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}$ General weighted harmonic mean. $\beta=2$ weights recall $2\times$ over precision. High-stakes medical triage where missing a disease is catastrophic.
Matthews Correlation (MCC) $\frac{\text{TP}\cdot\text{TN} - \text{FP}\cdot\text{FN}}{\sqrt{(\text{TP}+\text{FP})(\text{TP}+\text{FN})(\text{TN}+\text{FP})(\text{TN}+\text{FN})}}$ Pearson correlation between binary actuals and predictions ($[-1, +1]$). The single most robust scalar metric on severely imbalanced datasets.

2. Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) & The Mann-Whitney U Theorem

Binary classifiers output continuous confidence scores or probabilities $\hat{p} = f(\mathbf{x}) \in [0, 1]$. Applying a fixed decision threshold $\tau = 0.5$ creates a single confusion matrix. The ROC Curve visualizes classifier performance across all possible thresholds $\tau \in [0, 1]$ by plotting:

$$y\text{-axis: } \text{True Positive Rate (TPR)} = \frac{\text{TP}(\tau)}{\text{TP}(\tau) + \text{FN}(\tau)}, \quad x\text{-axis: } \text{False Positive Rate (FPR)} = \frac{\text{FP}(\tau)}{\text{TN}(\tau) + \text{FP}(\tau)}$$

A random guessing classifier produces the diagonal line $\text{TPR} = \text{FPR}$ with $\text{AUC} = 0.50$. A perfect classifier forms a right angle through $(0, 1)$ with $\text{AUC} = 1.00$.

🏆 The Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney Equivalence Theorem

Why is ROC-AUC universally favored in scoring algorithms? Because of an extraordinary mathematical equivalence: The Area Under the ROC Curve is identically equal to the probability that a randomly selected positive sample receives a higher score than a randomly selected negative sample!

$$\text{AUC} = P(S_+ > S_-) + \frac{1}{2} P(S_+ = S_-)$$

Where $S_+ \sim f(\mathbf{x} \mid y=1)$ and $S_- \sim f(\mathbf{x} \mid y=0)$.

Furthermore, AUC can be computed in $O(N \log N)$ time without integrating trapezoids, directly using the Mann-Whitney $U$ rank-sum statistic:

$$\text{AUC} = \frac{R_+ - \frac{N_+(N_+ + 1)}{2}}{N_+ N_-}$$

Where $R_+$ is the sum of ranks assigned to positive instances when all $N = N_+ + N_-$ samples are pooled and sorted in ascending order.

3. Precision-Recall Dynamics Under Severe Imbalance

While ROC-AUC is scale-invariant and threshold-independent, it exhibits a dangerous blind spot when negative cases vastly outnumber positives ($N_- \gg N_+$, such as ad click-through rates or cyber intrusion detection):

$$\text{FPR} = \frac{\text{FP}}{\text{TN} + \text{FP}} \approx \frac{\text{FP}}{N_-}$$

If $N_- = 10,000,000$ and a model produces $\text{FP} = 10,000$ false alarms, the False Positive Rate is a negligible $\text{FPR} = \frac{10,000}{10,000,000} = 0.001$ ($0.1\%$). The ROC curve stays hugged against the top-left corner, reporting an impressive $\text{AUC} = 0.98$!

However, if the positive cohort only had $N_+ = 100$ true fraud cases, and the model caught all of them ($\text{TP} = 100$), the Precision is:

$$\text{Precision} = \frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FP}} = \frac{100}{100 + 10,000} = \frac{100}{10,100} \approx 0.0099 \quad (0.99\%)$$

For every $1$ real fraudster caught, security analysts must investigate $100$ false alarms! The Precision-Recall (PR) Curve exposes this reality by plotting Precision vs. Recall across all thresholds. Unlike ROC curves whose baseline is always $0.50$, the baseline for a PR curve is the dataset prevalence $\pi = \frac{N_+}{N_+ + N_-}$. In severe imbalance, PR-AUC (Average Precision) is the mandatory evaluation metric.

4. Probability Calibration & Expected Calibration Error (ECE)

In high-stakes autonomous systems (self-driving cars, medical diagnosis, algorithmic trading), a model must not only predict the correct class—its predicted probability $\hat{p} \in [0, 1]$ must represent the true frequency of occurrence. If a weather model predicts an $80\%$ chance of rain on 100 separate days, it must rain on exactly 80 of those days.

Expected Calibration Error (ECE)

To quantify calibration, we partition predictions into $M$ equally spaced bins $B_1, \dots, B_M \subset [0, 1]$ (typically $M = 10$ or $15$). For each bin $B_m$:

$$\operatorname{acc}(B_m) = \frac{1}{|B_m|} \sum_{i \in B_m} \mathbb{I}(y_i = \hat{y}_i), \quad \operatorname{conf}(B_m) = \frac{1}{|B_m|} \sum_{i \in B_m} \hat{p}_i$$

Expected Calibration Error (ECE) is the weighted average absolute difference between confidence and accuracy:

$$\text{ECE} = \sum_{m=1}^M \frac{|B_m|}{N} |\operatorname{acc}(B_m) - \operatorname{conf}(B_m)|$$

The Brier Score

The Brier Score measures the mean squared error of predicted probabilities: $\text{BS} = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N (\hat{p}_i - y_i)^2$. Murphy (1973) proved that the Brier Score decomposes into three interpretable components:

$$\text{BS} = \text{Reliability (Calibration Error)} - \text{Resolution (Discrimination Power)} + \text{Uncertainty (Entropy)}$$

Post-Hoc Calibration Techniques

  • Platt Scaling (Sigmoid): Fits a univariate logistic regression on uncalibrated model logits $z_i$: $\hat{p}_{\text{cal}} = \sigma(A z_i + B)$. Parameters $A, B$ are fitted on validation data via log-loss. Ideal for SVMs and shallow networks.
  • Isotonic Regression: Fits a non-parametric, piece-wise constant monotonically non-decreasing step function. Free of parametric assumptions, but requires large validation sets ($N > 1,000$) to avoid overfitting.
  • Temperature Scaling: For multi-class deep networks, divides all pre-softmax logits by a learned scalar temperature $T > 0$: $\hat{p}_i = \frac{e^{z_i / T}}{\sum_j e^{z_j / T}}$. When $T > 1$, it softens overconfident probabilities without changing the $\arg\max$ top-1 predictions or accuracy!

Model Evaluation Practice Suite & Complete Worked Solutions

Problem 2.9.1 Level 1 • Direct Numerical Warm-Up Drill Google

QUESTION: A hospital AI diagnostic system is evaluated on a test cohort of $N = 1,000$ patients. The confusion matrix yields:

  • True Positives ($\text{TP}$): $40$
  • False Positives ($\text{FP}$): $60$
  • False Negatives ($\text{FN}$): $10$
  • True Negatives ($\text{TN}$): $890$
  1. Calculate the overall Classification Accuracy.
  2. Calculate Precision and Recall (Sensitivity).
  3. Calculate Specificity and False Positive Rate (FPR).
  4. Calculate the balanced $F_1$ score.
  5. Calculate the $F_2$ score (which weights recall twice as heavily as precision).
  6. Calculate the Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC).
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Arithmetic Solution

Step 1: Classification Accuracy:
$$\text{Accuracy} = \frac{\text{TP} + \text{TN}}{N} = \frac{40 + 890}{1000} = \frac{930}{1000} = 0.9300 \quad (93.00\%)$$

Step 2: Precision and Recall:
$$\text{Precision} = \frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FP}} = \frac{40}{40 + 60} = \frac{40}{100} = 0.4000 \quad (40.00\%)$$ $$\text{Recall} = \frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FN}} = \frac{40}{40 + 10} = \frac{40}{50} = 0.8000 \quad (80.00\%)$$

Step 3: Specificity and FPR:
$$\text{Specificity} = \frac{\text{TN}}{\text{TN} + \text{FP}} = \frac{890}{890 + 60} = \frac{890}{950} \approx 0.9368 \quad (93.68\%)$$ $$\text{FPR} = 1 - \text{Specificity} = \frac{60}{950} \approx 0.0632 \quad (6.32\%)$$

Step 4: $F_1$ Score:
$$F_1 = 2 \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}} = 2 \cdot \frac{0.40 \times 0.80}{0.40 + 0.80} = 2 \cdot \frac{0.32}{1.20} = \frac{0.64}{1.20} \approx 0.5333$$

Step 5: $F_2$ Score:
With $\beta = 2$, $\beta^2 = 4$: $$F_2 = (1 + 4) \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{4 \cdot \text{Precision} + \text{Recall}} = 5 \cdot \frac{0.40 \times 0.80}{4(0.40) + 0.80} = 5 \cdot \frac{0.32}{1.60 + 0.80} = \frac{1.60}{2.40} \approx 0.6667$$ Because recall ($0.80$) is higher than precision ($0.40$), the $F_2$ score rewards the model for prioritizing clinical sensitivity.

Step 6: Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC):
$$\text{Numerator} = (\text{TP} \cdot \text{TN}) - (\text{FP} \cdot \text{FN}) = (40 \times 890) - (60 \times 10) = 35600 - 600 = 35000$$ $$\text{Denominator} = \sqrt{(\text{TP}+\text{FP})(\text{TP}+\text{FN})(\text{TN}+\text{FP})(\text{TN}+\text{FN})}$$ $$\text{Denominator} = \sqrt{(100)(50)(950)(900)} = \sqrt{4,275,000,000} \approx 65383.48$$ $$\text{MCC} = \frac{35000}{65383.48} \approx 0.5353$$ An MCC of $0.5353$ indicates moderate positive correlation, exposing the fact that despite a superficial $93\%$ accuracy, the model has significant false positive noise.

Problem 2.9.2 Level 1 • Direct Formula Drill Meta

QUESTION: A machine learning weather forecasting model predicts the probability of rainfall $\hat{p}_i$ for 4 consecutive days, with observed binary outcomes $y_i \in \{0, 1\}$:

DayPredicted Probability $\hat{p}_i$Ground Truth Outcome $y_i$
Day 1$0.90$$1$ (Rain)
Day 2$0.80$$1$ (Rain)
Day 3$0.40$$0$ (No Rain)
Day 4$0.10$$0$ (No Rain)
  1. Calculate the exact Brier Score of the model: $\text{BS} = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N (\hat{p}_i - y_i)^2$.
  2. Calculate the Brier Score of a naive baseline model that always predicts the climatological sample mean $\bar{y}$ for all days.
  3. Compute the Brier Skill Score (BSS) of the model relative to the climatological baseline: $\text{BSS} = 1 - \frac{\text{BS}_{\text{model}}}{\text{BS}_{\text{ref}}}$.
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Mathematical Solution

Step 1: Compute Model Brier Score:
For each observation, calculate squared error $(\hat{p}_i - y_i)^2$:

  • Day 1: $(0.90 - 1)^2 = (-0.10)^2 = 0.0100$
  • Day 2: $(0.80 - 1)^2 = (-0.20)^2 = 0.0400$
  • Day 3: $(0.40 - 0)^2 = (0.40)^2 = 0.1600$
  • Day 4: $(0.10 - 0)^2 = (0.10)^2 = 0.0100$
$$\text{BS}_{\text{model}} = \frac{0.0100 + 0.0400 + 0.1600 + 0.0100}{4} = \frac{0.2200}{4} = 0.0550$$

Step 2: Compute Climatological Baseline Brier Score:
The base rate of rain in the dataset is: $$\bar{y} = \frac{1 + 1 + 0 + 0}{4} = \frac{2}{4} = 0.50$$ The baseline predicts $\hat{p}_{\text{ref}} = 0.50$ for every day:

  • Day 1: $(0.50 - 1)^2 = (-0.50)^2 = 0.25$
  • Day 2: $(0.50 - 1)^2 = (-0.50)^2 = 0.25$
  • Day 3: $(0.50 - 0)^2 = (0.50)^2 = 0.25$
  • Day 4: $(0.50 - 0)^2 = (0.50)^2 = 0.25$
$$\text{BS}_{\text{ref}} = \frac{0.25 + 0.25 + 0.25 + 0.25}{4} = 0.2500$$

Step 3: Compute Brier Skill Score (BSS):
$$\text{BSS} = 1 - \frac{\text{BS}_{\text{model}}}{\text{BS}_{\text{ref}}} = 1 - \frac{0.0550}{0.2500} = 1 - 0.2200 = 0.7800 \quad (78.00\%)$$ A Brier Skill Score of $+0.78$ demonstrates that the model reduces probabilistic forecast variance by $78\%$ compared to naive climatological guessing.

Problem 2.9.3 Level 2 • Applied Engineering Trace Amazon

QUESTION: You are given a validation set of $N = 6$ samples with predicted probabilities $\hat{p}_i$ and ground-truth labels $y_i$:

Sample$\hat{p}_i$Actual Label $y_i$
$s_1$$0.95$$1$
$s_2$$0.85$$1$
$s_3$$0.70$$0$
$s_4$$0.60$$1$
$s_5$$0.40$$0$
$s_6$$0.15$$0$

Note that $N_+ = 3$ and $N_- = 3$.

  1. Construct the full ROC threshold sweep table by placing decision thresholds between adjacent unique probability scores: $\tau \in \{1.0, 0.90, 0.75, 0.65, 0.50, 0.25, 0.0\}$. For each threshold, compute (TP, FP, TN, FN, TPR, FPR).
  2. Plot the sequence of $(\text{FPR}, \text{TPR})$ coordinates in ROC space.
  3. Compute the exact Area Under the ROC Curve (ROC-AUC) using the trapezoidal numerical integration rule.
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Algorithmic Solution

Step 1: Construct the Full Threshold Sweep Table:
Classification rule: $\hat{y}_i = 1$ if $\hat{p}_i \ge \tau$, else $0$. Total positives $P = 3$, total negatives $N = 3$.
$\text{TPR} = \frac{\text{TP}}{3}$, $\text{FPR} = \frac{\text{FP}}{3}$:
Threshold $\tau$Positive Predictions ($\hat{p} \ge \tau$)TPFPTNFNFPRTPRPoint
$\tau > 0.95$$\emptyset$$0$$0$$3$$3$$0.000$$0.000$$(0, 0)$
$\tau = 0.90$$\{s_1\}$$1$$0$$3$$2$$0.000$$\frac{1}{3} \approx 0.333$$(0, \frac{1}{3})$
$\tau = 0.75$$\{s_1, s_2\}$$2$$0$$3$$1$$0.000$$\frac{2}{3} \approx 0.667$$(0, \frac{2}{3})$
$\tau = 0.65$$\{s_1, s_2, s_3\}$$2$$1$$2$$1$$\frac{1}{3} \approx 0.333$$\frac{2}{3} \approx 0.667$$(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{2}{3})$
$\tau = 0.50$$\{s_1, s_2, s_3, s_4\}$$3$$1$$2$$0$$\frac{1}{3} \approx 0.333$$\frac{3}{3} = 1.000$$(\frac{1}{3}, 1)$
$\tau = 0.25$$\{s_1, s_2, s_3, s_4, s_5\}$$3$$2$$1$$0$$\frac{2}{3} \approx 0.667$$\frac{3}{3} = 1.000$$(\frac{2}{3}, 1)$
$\tau = 0.00$All $\{s_1, \dots, s_6\}$$3$$3$$0$$0$$1.000$$1.000$$(1, 1)$

Step 2: Sequence of Points:
The operating points in ROC space are: $$P_0 = (0, 0) \to P_1 = \left(0, \frac{1}{3}\right) \to P_2 = \left(0, \frac{2}{3}\right) \to P_3 = \left(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{2}{3}\right) \to P_4 = \left(\frac{1}{3}, 1\right) \to P_5 = \left(\frac{2}{3}, 1\right) \to P_6 = (1, 1)$$

Step 3: Trapezoidal Numerical Integration:
The curve moves along rectangular segments. Area under the curve: $$\text{AUC} = \sum_{k=1}^6 \frac{\text{TPR}_k + \text{TPR}_{k-1}}{2} \cdot (\text{FPR}_k - \text{FPR}_{k-1})$$ Examining where $\Delta \text{FPR} > 0$:

  • Segment $P_2 \to P_3$: $\Delta \text{FPR} = \frac{1}{3} - 0 = \frac{1}{3}$. Height is constant at $\text{TPR} = \frac{2}{3}$.
    $$\text{Area}_1 = \frac{2}{3} \times \frac{1}{3} = \frac{2}{9}$$
  • Segment $P_4 \to P_5$: $\Delta \text{FPR} = \frac{2}{3} - \frac{1}{3} = \frac{1}{3}$. Height is constant at $\text{TPR} = 1.0$.
    $$\text{Area}_2 = 1.0 \times \frac{1}{3} = \frac{1}{3} = \frac{3}{9}$$
  • Segment $P_5 \to P_6$: $\Delta \text{FPR} = 1.0 - \frac{2}{3} = \frac{1}{3}$. Height is constant at $\text{TPR} = 1.0$.
    $$\text{Area}_3 = 1.0 \times \frac{1}{3} = \frac{1}{3} = \frac{3}{9}$$
Sum of all trapezoidal slices: $$\text{AUC} = \frac{2}{9} + \frac{3}{9} + \frac{3}{9} = \frac{8}{9} \approx 0.8889 \quad (88.89\%)$$

Problem 2.9.4 Level 2 • Algorithmic Tracing & Rank-Sum Apple

QUESTION: Using the exact same 6 samples from Problem 2.9.3 ($\hat{p} = [0.95, 0.85, 0.70, 0.60, 0.40, 0.15]$ and $y = [1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0]$):

  1. Sort all 6 samples in ascending order of their predicted scores $\hat{p}_i$ and assign ranks from $1$ (lowest score) to $6$ (highest score).
  2. Calculate the rank-sum of the positive instances: $R_+ = \sum_{i: y_i = 1} \text{Rank}(s_i)$.
  3. Compute the Mann-Whitney $U$ statistic: $U = R_+ - \frac{N_+(N_+ + 1)}{2}$.
  4. Calculate $\text{AUC} = \frac{U}{N_+ N_-}$ and verify that it matches the trapezoidal area $\frac{8}{9}$ from Problem 2.9.3 identically.
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Rank-Sum Solution

Step 1: Sort and Assign Ascending Ranks:
Arranging from lowest to highest score:
SampleScore $\hat{p}_i$Actual Label $y_i$Rank ($1$ to $6$)
$s_6$$0.15$$0$ (Neg)$1$
$s_5$$0.40$$0$ (Neg)$2$
$s_4$$0.60$$1$ (Pos)$3$
$s_3$$0.70$$0$ (Neg)$4$
$s_2$$0.85$$1$ (Pos)$5$
$s_1$$0.95$$1$ (Pos)$6$

Step 2: Calculate Positive Rank-Sum $R_+$:
The positive samples are $\{s_4, s_2, s_1\}$: $$R_+ = \text{Rank}(s_4) + \text{Rank}(s_2) + \text{Rank}(s_1) = 3 + 5 + 6 = 14$$

Step 3: Compute Mann-Whitney $U$ Statistic:
With $N_+ = 3$: $$\frac{N_+(N_+ + 1)}{2} = \frac{3 \times 4}{2} = 6$$ $$U = R_+ - 6 = 14 - 6 = 8$$

Step 4: Compute AUC:
With $N_+ = 3$ and $N_- = 3$, the total number of positive-negative pairs is $N_+ \times N_- = 3 \times 3 = 9$: $$\text{AUC} = \frac{U}{N_+ N_-} = \frac{8}{9} \approx 0.8889$$ Equivalence Verification: Let us count the number of concordant pairs directly:

  • Positive $s_1$ ($0.95$) beats all 3 negatives ($s_3, s_5, s_6$): $3$ wins.
  • Positive $s_2$ ($0.85$) beats all 3 negatives ($s_3, s_5, s_6$): $3$ wins.
  • Positive $s_4$ ($0.60$) beats $s_5$ ($0.40$) and $s_6$ ($0.15$), but loses to $s_3$ ($0.70$): $2$ wins.
Total pairwise wins: $3 + 3 + 2 = 8$ out of $9$ pairs! Thus: $$P(S_+ > S_-) = \frac{8}{9} = \text{AUC}_{\text{trapezoid}} \equiv \text{AUC}_{\text{Mann-Whitney}}$$ The rank-sum method computes the exact AUC in $O(N \log N)$ sorting time, completely avoiding threshold interpolation errors.

Problem 2.9.5 Level 3 • FAANG / Research Lab Proof Google Meta

QUESTION: Prove rigorously that for continuous prediction scores $S$, the Area Under the ROC Curve is mathematically equivalent to the probability that a randomly chosen positive sample receives a higher score than a randomly chosen negative sample: $$\text{AUC} = P(S_+ > S_-)$$

✓ Complete Mathematical Proof via Fubini's Theorem

Step 1: Probability Definitions in ROC Space:
Let $S_+$ and $S_-$ be random variables representing the scores assigned to positive ($Y=1$) and negative ($Y=0$) instances respectively, with probability density functions $f_+(s)$ and $f_-(s)$, and cumulative distribution functions $F_+(s)$ and $F_-(s)$.
For any threshold $\tau \in (-\infty, \infty)$: $$\text{TPR}(\tau) = P(S_+ \ge \tau) = 1 - F_+(\tau) = \int_{\tau}^{\infty} f_+(s) \, ds$$ $$\text{FPR}(\tau) = P(S_- \ge \tau) = 1 - F_-(\tau) = \int_{\tau}^{\infty} f_-(s) \, ds$$

Step 2: Formulate AUC as an Integral:
The area under the ROC curve is the integral of $\text{TPR}$ with respect to $\text{FPR}$ as the threshold sweeps from $\tau = \infty$ ($\text{FPR} = 0$) to $\tau = -\infty$ ($\text{FPR} = 1$): $$\text{AUC} = \int_{0}^{1} \text{TPR} \, d(\text{FPR})$$ Perform a change of variables from $\text{FPR}$ to the threshold parameter $\tau$.
Since $\text{FPR}(\tau) = 1 - F_-(\tau)$, the differential is: $$d(\text{FPR}(\tau)) = -f_-(\tau) \, d\tau$$ When $\text{FPR} = 0 \implies \tau = \infty$, and when $\text{FPR} = 1 \implies \tau = -\infty$.

Step 3: Integrate Over the Score Support:
Substituting into the AUC integral: $$\text{AUC} = \int_{\infty}^{-\infty} \text{TPR}(\tau) \cdot (-f_-(\tau) \, d\tau) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \text{TPR}(\tau) f_-(\tau) \, d\tau$$ Now substitute the definition of $\text{TPR}(\tau) = P(S_+ > \tau)$: $$\text{AUC} = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} P(S_+ > \tau) f_-(\tau) \, d\tau$$

Step 4: Recognize the Law of Total Probability:
Notice that $f_-(\tau) \, d\tau$ is the probability density that the negative score $S_-$ takes value $\tau$.
The integral $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} P(S_+ > \tau) f_-(\tau) \, d\tau$ is precisely the expected value: $$\mathbb{E}_{S_-} [P(S_+ > S_- \mid S_-)] = P(S_+ > S_-)$$ Thus: $$\text{AUC} = P(S_+ > S_-)$$ This completes the proof. The Area Under the ROC Curve is the exact non-parametric probability that the classifier ranks an arbitrary positive instance higher than an arbitrary negative instance. $\blacksquare$

Problem 2.9.6 Level 3 • FAANG / Research Lab Proof Google Amazon

QUESTION: In clinical triage, failing to detect a life-threatening infection (False Negative) is substantially worse than ordering an unnecessary blood test (False Positive). Starting from a generalized weighted harmonic mean formulation between Precision and Recall, derive the mathematical formula for the $F_\beta$ score and prove why setting $\beta = 2$ weights Recall twice as heavily as Precision.

✓ Complete First-Principles Derivation

Step 1: Weighted Harmonic Mean Formulation:
The weighted harmonic mean $H$ of two positive quantities $P$ (Precision) and $R$ (Recall) with non-negative weights $w_P$ and $w_R$ (where $w_P + w_R = 1$) is defined as: $$\frac{1}{H} = \frac{w_P \frac{1}{P} + w_R \frac{1}{R}}{w_P + w_R} = w_P \frac{1}{P} + w_R \frac{1}{R}$$ Inverting both sides: $$H = \frac{1}{\frac{w_P}{P} + \frac{w_R}{R}} = \frac{P \cdot R}{w_R P + w_P R}$$

Step 2: Parameterize Relative Weight via $\beta$:
We define $\beta \in (0, \infty)$ such that Recall is considered $\beta$ times as important as Precision. In terms of weight allocation: $$\frac{w_R}{w_P} = \beta^2 \implies w_R = \beta^2 w_P$$ Since $w_P + w_R = 1$: $$w_P + \beta^2 w_P = 1 \implies w_P(1 + \beta^2) = 1 \implies w_P = \frac{1}{1 + \beta^2}$$ $$w_R = \frac{\beta^2}{1 + \beta^2}$$

Step 3: Substitute Weights into Harmonic Mean:
$$\frac{1}{F_\beta} = \left(\frac{1}{1 + \beta^2}\right) \frac{1}{P} + \left(\frac{\beta^2}{1 + \beta^2}\right) \frac{1}{R}$$ $$\frac{1}{F_\beta} = \frac{1}{1 + \beta^2} \left[ \frac{1}{P} + \frac{\beta^2}{R} \right] = \frac{1}{1 + \beta^2} \left[ \frac{R + \beta^2 P}{P \cdot R} \right]$$ Taking the reciprocal: $$F_\beta = (1 + \beta^2) \frac{P \cdot R}{\beta^2 P + R}$$ Multiplying and rearranging in standard textbook form: $$F_\beta = (1 + \beta^2) \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\beta^2 \text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}$$

Step 4: Mathematical Behavior for $\beta = 2$:
When $\beta = 2$: $$F_2 = (1 + 4) \frac{P \cdot R}{4 P + R} = 5 \frac{P \cdot R}{4 P + R}$$ Examine the partial derivatives to see sensitivity: $$\frac{\partial (1/F_\beta)}{\partial (1/R)} = \frac{\beta^2}{1 + \beta^2}, \quad \frac{\partial (1/F_\beta)}{\partial (1/P)} = \frac{1}{1 + \beta^2}$$ The ratio of partial derivatives is: $$\frac{\frac{\partial (1/F_\beta)}{\partial (1/R)}}{\frac{\partial (1/F_\beta)}{\partial (1/P)}} = \beta^2 = 2^2 = 4$$ In terms of relative error penalties, a drop in Recall penalizes the $F_2$ score $4\times$ more severely in inverse harmonic terms, which mathematically reflects valuing Recall twice as much ($\beta = 2$) as Precision. $\blacksquare$

Problem 2.9.7 Level 4 • Conceptual Trap MCQ Google Meta

QUESTION: An AI system for cyber security intrusion detection monitors $1,000,000$ daily network requests, of which exactly $100$ are malicious cyber attacks ($0.01\%$ prevalence). The validation pipeline reports an outstanding $\text{ROC-AUC} = 0.980$. However, when deployed, the network security operations center (SOC) complains that the tool is unusable because over $95\%$ of alerts generated are benign false alarms. Why did ROC-AUC fail to diagnose this issue?

  1. ROC-AUC was calculated using the trapezoidal rule instead of Simpson's rule, inflating the metric by $50\%$.
  2. In ROC curves, $\text{FPR} = \frac{\text{FP}}{\text{TN} + \text{FP}}$ has total negatives ($\approx 1,000,000$) in the denominator. A small FPR of $0.002$ produces $2,000$ false alarms; since there are only $100$ true attacks, Precision collapses to $< 5\%$ despite a near-perfect ROC curve.
  3. ROC-AUC is undefined for datasets where prevalence is less than $1\%$, causing NaN values that scikit-learn defaults to $0.980$.
  4. The test data suffered from label flipping where malicious attacks were accidentally labeled as $0$.
✓ Correct Answer: B • Comprehensive Distractor Analysis

Detailed Analysis:
This is the single most common real-world failure mode in evaluating models under extreme class imbalance ($N_+ \ll N_-$).
The False Positive Rate is defined as: $$\text{FPR} = \frac{\text{FP}}{N_-}$$ Here, $N_- = 999,900 \approx 10^6$.
Suppose the model operates at an exceptional operating point with $\text{TPR} = 90\%$ ($90$ out of $100$ attacks caught) and a seemingly negligible $\text{FPR} = 0.2\% = 0.002$.
In ROC space, $(0.002, 0.90)$ is extremely close to the ideal top-left corner $(0, 1)$, yielding an overall $\text{ROC-AUC} \approx 0.98$.
However, let us calculate the absolute number of False Positives: $$\text{FP} = \text{FPR} \times N_- = 0.002 \times 999,900 \approx 2,000 \text{ false alarms!}$$ The resulting Precision is: $$\text{Precision} = \frac{\text{TP}}{\text{TP} + \text{FP}} = \frac{90}{90 + 2000} = \frac{90}{2090} \approx 0.043 \quad (4.3\%)$$ Out of every 100 alerts sent to the security team, 96 are false alarms! The security engineers suffer from alert fatigue and turn off the system.
Lesson: Under severe class imbalance, ROC-AUC is misleadingly optimistic because large $N_-$ masks high absolute FP counts. Precision-Recall AUC (PR-AUC) must always be reported.

Why Other Options are False:

  • A is FALSE: Integration methods (trapezoidal vs. Simpson's) differ by at most $0.0001$, not $50\%$.
  • C is FALSE: ROC-AUC is mathematically well-defined for any dataset having at least 1 positive and 1 negative sample ($N_+ \ge 1, N_- \ge 1$).
  • D is FALSE: Label flipping would decrease both ROC-AUC and Precision; the issue here is the mathematical nature of the FPR denominator under massive negative sample size.

Problem 2.9.8 Level 4 • Assertion-Reasoning & Temperature Scaling Google OpenAI

QUESTION: Evaluate the following Assertion and Reason regarding calibration in deep neural networks:

Assertion (A): Applying Temperature Scaling $\hat{p}_i = \frac{e^{z_i / T}}{\sum_{j} e^{z_j / T}}$ with an optimal scalar $T > 1$ to the pre-softmax logits $z$ of an overconfident deep neural network dramatically reduces Expected Calibration Error (ECE) while leaving the model's top-1 classification accuracy and ROC-AUC completely unchanged.

Reason (R): Dividing all logits by a strictly positive scalar constant $T > 0$ is a strictly monotonic transformation that preserves the ordinal ranking of logits ($\arg\max_k z_k = \arg\max_k \frac{z_k}{T}$), ensuring that decision boundaries and pairwise orderings are invariant while the softmax probabilities are softened toward empirical accuracy.

  1. Both (A) and (R) are true, and (R) is the correct mathematical explanation of (A).
  2. Both (A) and (R) are true, but (R) is NOT the correct explanation of (A).
  3. (A) is true, but (R) is false.
  4. (A) is false, but (R) is true.
✓ Correct Answer: A • Comprehensive Invariance Proof

Detailed Analysis:
Analysis of Reason (R):
Consider pre-softmax logits $\mathbf{z} = [z_1, \dots, z_K]^T \in \mathbb{R}^K$.
For any scalar $T > 0$, the function $g(z) = \frac{z}{T}$ has derivative $g'(z) = \frac{1}{T} > 0$, making it a strictly monotonically increasing function.
Therefore: $$z_i > z_j \iff \frac{z_i}{T} > \frac{z_j}{T}$$ The $\arg\max$ operation is invariant: $$\arg\max_{k \in \{1,\dots,K\}} \frac{e^{z_k / T}}{\sum_j e^{z_j / T}} = \arg\max_{k \in \{1,\dots,K\}} \frac{z_k}{T} = \arg\max_{k \in \{1,\dots,K\}} z_k$$ The predicted top-1 class is identical for every input sample. Furthermore, for binary classification where score $S = \sigma(z / T)$, the pairwise ranking between any two samples $a$ and $b$ is preserved: $z_a > z_b \iff \sigma(z_a / T) > \sigma(z_b / T)$. Reason (R) is true.

Analysis of Assertion (A):
Because the top-1 predicted class is invariant for every sample, the overall classification accuracy is strictly identical before and after temperature scaling.
Because the pairwise orderings of scores are invariant ($P(S_a > S_b)$ is unchanged), by the Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney theorem, the ROC-AUC is strictly identical.
However, modern deep neural networks (Guo et al., 2017) are severely overconfident due to negative log-likelihood training with batch normalization and weight decay: they output confidence $\hat{p} = 0.99$ for predictions that only have $85\%$ empirical accuracy.
Setting $T > 1$ pulls the softmax outputs away from the extreme boundaries $\{0, 1\}$ and toward the uniform distribution, aligning predicted probabilities $\hat{p}$ with observed accuracy and drastically slashing Expected Calibration Error (ECE) from $15\%$ down to $< 2\%$.
Thus, (A) is true, and (R) provides the exact mathematical justification. $\blacksquare$

5. Production Code Lab 2.9: Fast Mann-Whitney ROC-AUC & Calibration Engine

Below is a production-grade Python engine that computes the Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney ROC-AUC in $O(N \log N)$ time and evaluates Expected Calibration Error (ECE).

Code Lab 2.9 — High-Performance Evaluation & Calibration Engine

🔗 View in GitHub: part2_classical_ml/ch29_model_evaluation/evaluation_engine.py

import numpy as np

def fast_roc_auc(y_true, y_scores):
    """
    Computes exact ROC-AUC via the Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney rank-sum theorem.
    Time Complexity: O(N log N) dominated by sorting.
    """
    y_true = np.asarray(y_true)
    y_scores = np.asarray(y_scores)
    
    pos_mask = (y_true == 1)
    n_pos = np.sum(pos_mask)
    n_neg = len(y_true) - n_pos
    
    if n_pos == 0 or n_neg == 0:
        raise ValueError("Both positive and negative samples required to compute ROC-AUC.")
        
    # Sort scores in ascending order and rank them (1-indexed)
    order = np.argsort(y_scores)
    ranks = np.empty_like(order, dtype=float)
    ranks[order] = np.arange(1, len(y_scores) + 1)
    
    # Handle tied scores by averaging their ranks
    unique_scores, inv_idx, counts = np.unique(y_scores, return_inverse=True, return_counts=True)
    tied_scores = unique_scores[counts > 1]
    for s in tied_scores:
        ties = (y_scores == s)
        ranks[ties] = np.mean(ranks[ties])
        
    # Wilcoxon U statistic
    r_pos = np.sum(ranks[pos_mask])
    u_stat = r_pos - (n_pos * (n_pos + 1)) / 2.0
    return u_stat / (n_pos * n_neg)

def expected_calibration_error(y_true, y_probs, n_bins=10):
    """
    Computes Expected Calibration Error (ECE) across equally spaced bins.
    """
    y_true = np.asarray(y_true)
    y_probs = np.asarray(y_probs)
    bin_limits = np.linspace(0.0, 1.0, n_bins + 1)
    
    ece = 0.0
    N = len(y_true)
    
    for i in range(n_bins):
        bin_lower = bin_limits[i]
        bin_upper = bin_limits[i + 1]
        
        # Binned indices
        in_bin = (y_probs >= bin_lower) & (y_probs < bin_upper if i < n_bins - 1 else y_probs <= bin_upper)
        n_in_bin = np.sum(in_bin)
        
        if n_in_bin > 0:
            bin_acc = np.mean(y_true[in_bin])
            bin_conf = np.mean(y_probs[in_bin])
            ece += (n_in_bin / N) * np.abs(bin_acc - bin_conf)
            
    return ece

if __name__ == "__main__":
    np.random.seed(42)
    y_actual = np.array([1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0])
    y_pred_scores = np.array([0.95, 0.85, 0.70, 0.60, 0.40, 0.15, 0.88, 0.35, 0.72, 0.20])
    
    auc = fast_roc_auc(y_actual, y_pred_scores)
    ece = expected_calibration_error(y_actual, y_pred_scores, n_bins=5)
    
    print(f"Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney ROC-AUC : {auc:.4f}")
    print(f"Expected Calibration Error   : {ece:.4f}")
Chapter 2.9 Dedicated Project & Case Study

Project 2.9: Production High-Prevalence Fraud Evaluation & Recalibration Suite

Architect and deploy a real-time model evaluation telemetry service that streams predictions, detects calibration drift, computes running PR-AUC, and applies online Platt Scaling updates without service interruption.

🔗 Full Project Code: part2_classical_ml/ch29_model_evaluation/evaluation_engine.py

Case Study Architecture: In high-throughput banking systems (Adyen, PayPal), models process millions of transactions per hour with 0.005% fraud base rates. If an upstream data feed changes (e.g., introduction of a new mobile wallet payment rail), model calibration degrades rapidly even if ranking AUC remains high. In this project, you construct an automated streaming evaluator that tracks Expected Calibration Error in rolling 1-hour windows and triggers automated temperature recalibration when ECE exceeds 5%.

Chapter 2.10

Reinforcement Learning: From Bellman Equations to Deep Q-Networks

MDPs, Bellman Contraction Mapping, Policy Gradients, DQN & PPO ✦

"The hallmark of intelligence is not whether you can learn from a teacher, but whether you can learn from trial and error."

— Richard Sutton & Andrew Barto, Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction

Foundation • Core Pillar

1. The Third Paradigm of Machine Learning

Supervised learning requires ground-truth labels $(\mathbf{x}, y)$; unsupervised learning discovers latent geometric structure without labels. Reinforcement Learning (RL) is the third fundamental paradigm: an agent learns an optimal sequence of decisions by interacting with an environment, receiving scalar rewards for favorable actions and penalties for mistakes, without any external teacher specifying the exact optimal action.

2. Markov Decision Processes (MDPs)

The formal mathematical framework for reinforcement learning is the Markov Decision Process (MDP), defined by the 5-tuple $(\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{A}, P, R, \gamma)$:

Figure 2.10.1: The canonical Reinforcement Learning interaction cycle. The agent observes current state $S_t$, takes action $A_t$ sampled from policy $\pi$, and receives scalar reward $R_{t+1}$ and next state $S_{t+1}$ from the environment transition dynamics $\mathcal{P}$.

  • $\mathcal{S}$: State Space — the set of all environment configurations.
  • $\mathcal{A}$: Action Space — the set of all legal agent actions.
  • $P(s' \mid s, a)$: Transition Probability — $P(s_{t+1} = s' \mid s_t = s, a_t = a)$.
  • $R(s, a, s')$: Reward Function — scalar feedback $r_{t+1} \in \mathbb{R}$.
  • $\gamma \in [0, 1)$: Discount Factor — exponential temporal weighting for future rewards.

The Markov Property:
The future transition depends exclusively on the current state and action, completely conditional on the immediate present: $$P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t, s_{t-1}, a_{t-1}, \dots, s_0, a_0) = P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$$ State $s_t$ is a sufficient statistic for the entire interaction history. The agent does not need to memorize past trajectories to make optimal decisions.

Cumulative Discounted Return

The objective of the agent is to select actions that maximize the expected cumulative discounted return $G_t$:

$$G_t = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \gamma^k r_{t+k+1} = r_{t+1} + \gamma r_{t+2} + \gamma^2 r_{t+3} + \dots$$

Because $\gamma < 1$ and rewards are bounded ($|r| \le R_{\max}$), the infinite geometric series is guaranteed to converge: $G_t \le \frac{R_{\max}}{1 - \gamma}$.

3. Value Functions & The Bellman Equations

A policy $\pi(a \mid s)$ defines the probability distribution over actions given state $s$. We evaluate policies using two value functions:

  • State-Value Function $V^\pi(s)$: Expected return starting from state $s$ following policy $\pi$: $$V^\pi(s) = \mathbb{E}_\pi [G_t \mid s_t = s]$$
  • Action-Value Function $Q^\pi(s, a)$: Expected return starting from state $s$, taking action $a$, and thereafter following $\pi$: $$Q^\pi(s, a) = \mathbb{E}_\pi [G_t \mid s_t = s, a_t = a]$$

🏆 The Bellman Expectation and Optimality Equations

Decomposing $G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1}$ yields the recursive Bellman Expectation Equations:

$$V^\pi(s) = \sum_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \pi(a|s) \sum_{s' \in \mathcal{S}} P(s'|s,a) \left[ R(s,a,s') + \gamma V^\pi(s') \right]$$ $$Q^\pi(s, a) = \sum_{s' \in \mathcal{S}} P(s'|s,a) \left[ R(s,a,s') + \gamma \sum_{a'} \pi(a'|s') Q^\pi(s', a') \right]$$

For the optimal policy $\pi^* = \arg\max_\pi V^\pi(s)$, the value satisfies the non-linear Bellman Optimality Equations:

$$V^*(s) = \max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \sum_{s' \in \mathcal{S}} P(s'|s,a) \left[ R(s,a,s') + \gamma V^*(s') \right]$$ $$Q^*(s, a) = \sum_{s' \in \mathcal{S}} P(s'|s,a) \left[ R(s,a,s') + \gamma \max_{a' \in \mathcal{A}} Q^*(s', a') \right]$$

Once $Q^*(s, a)$ is determined, the optimal deterministic policy is simply greedy: $\pi^*(s) = \arg\max_{a} Q^*(s, a)$.

4. Model-Free Temporal Difference: Q-Learning

When the transition model $P(s' \mid s, a)$ is unknown, Q-Learning (Watkins, 1989) learns $Q^*(s, a)$ directly from transition samples $(s_t, a_t, r_{t+1}, s_{t+1})$ via Temporal Difference (TD) updates:

$$Q(s_t, a_t) \leftarrow Q(s_t, a_t) + \alpha \left[ \underbrace{r_{t+1} + \gamma \max_{a'} Q(s_{t+1}, a')}_{\text{TD Target}} - \underbrace{Q(s_t, a_t)}_{\text{Current Estimate}} \right]$$

Where $\delta_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma \max_{a'} Q(s_{t+1}, a') - Q(s_t, a_t)$ is the TD Error.

Off-Policy Nature: Q-Learning is an off-policy algorithm because the update target evaluates the greedy action $\max_{a'} Q(s_{t+1}, a')$ under the target policy $\pi^*$, regardless of which action $a_{t+1}$ the agent actually executes using its $\epsilon$-greedy exploratory behavior policy.

5. Deep Q-Networks (DQN): Mnih et al., DeepMind 2015

When state spaces are continuous or high-dimensional (such as raw video frames $84 \times 84 \times 4$), tabular representations become impossible. DQN parameterizes $Q(s, a; \theta)$ with a deep neural network. Naive gradient descent on consecutive transitions causes catastrophic divergence due to the Deadly Triad (Function Approximation + Bootstrapping + Off-Policy Learning). DQN resolves this with two architectural breakthroughs:

  1. Experience Replay Buffer $\mathcal{D}$: Transitions $(s_t, a_t, r_{t+1}, s_{t+1})$ are stored in a circular buffer. Mini-batches are uniformly sampled at random during training, breaking temporal correlations and restoring i.i.d. SGD assumptions.
  2. Separate Target Network $Q(s, a; \theta^-)$: A separate parameter set $\theta^-$ is held fixed and only synchronized periodically ($\theta^- \leftarrow \theta$ every $C$ steps). The loss function is: $$\mathcal{L}(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{(s, a, r, s') \sim \mathcal{D}} \left[ \left( r + \gamma \max_{a'} Q(s', a'; \theta^-) - Q(s, a; \theta) \right)^2 \right]$$

6. Policy Gradients & Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)

Value-based methods struggle with continuous action spaces (e.g., robotic joint torques $\mathcal{A} \subset \mathbb{R}^6$). Policy Gradient methods directly parameterize $\pi_\theta(a \mid s)$ and perform gradient ascent on expected return $J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\tau \sim \pi_\theta} [R(\tau)]$.

The Policy Gradient Theorem (Sutton et al., 2000)

$$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta} \left[ \sum_{t=0}^T \nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) \cdot A^{\pi_\theta}(s_t, a_t) \right]$$

Where $A(s, a) = Q(s, a) - V(s)$ is the Advantage Function, which drastically reduces gradient variance compared to raw returns $G_t$.

Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO): Schulman et al., 2017

Standard policy gradient steps can destructively collapse policy entropy. PPO constrains policy drift using the Clipped Surrogate Objective:

$$r_t(\theta) = \frac{\pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t)}{\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(a_t \mid s_t)}$$ $$\mathcal{L}^{\text{CLIP}}(\theta) = \hat{\mathbb{E}}_t \left[ \min\left( r_t(\theta) \hat{A}_t, \; \operatorname{clip}(r_t(\theta), 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon) \hat{A}_t \right) \right]$$

This clipped objective acts as a pessimistic bound, preventing large updates when the probability ratio $r_t(\theta)$ deviates beyond $[1-\epsilon, 1+\epsilon]$ (typically $\epsilon = 0.2$). PPO is the workhorse algorithm behind RLHF in modern LLMs (Chapter 5.6).

Reinforcement Learning Practice Suite & Complete Worked Solutions

Problem 2.10.1 Level 1 • Direct Formula & Discounted Return Drill Google DeepMind

QUESTION: An autonomous agent interacts with an episodic environment with discount factor $\gamma = 0.8$. An episode terminates after $4$ steps, generating the following sequence of rewards: $$r_1 = -1.0, \quad r_2 = 0.0, \quad r_3 = +2.0, \quad r_4 = +10.0$$ Calculate the exact discounted cumulative return $G_t = \sum_{k=0}^{T-t-1} \gamma^k r_{t+k+1}$ for all timesteps $t = 0, 1, 2, 3$.

✓ Complete Step-by-Step Arithmetic Solution

Step 1: Backward Recursive Formula:
Recall that returns satisfy the backward recursion: $$G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1}, \quad \text{with } G_T = G_4 = 0$$

Step 2: Calculate Returns from $t = 3$ back to $t = 0$:

  • For $t = 3$: $$G_3 = r_4 = +10.0$$
  • For $t = 2$: $$G_2 = r_3 + \gamma G_3 = 2.0 + (0.8)(10.0) = 2.0 + 8.0 = +10.0$$
  • For $t = 1$: $$G_1 = r_2 + \gamma G_2 = 0.0 + (0.8)(10.0) = 0.0 + 8.0 = +8.0$$
  • For $t = 0$: $$G_0 = r_1 + \gamma G_1 = -1.0 + (0.8)(8.0) = -1.0 + 6.4 = +5.4$$

Step 3: Verification via Direct Expansion:
$$G_0 = r_1 + \gamma r_2 + \gamma^2 r_3 + \gamma^3 r_4$$ $$G_0 = -1.0 + (0.8)(0.0) + (0.64)(2.0) + (0.512)(10.0) = -1.0 + 0.0 + 1.28 + 5.12 = 5.40$$ The results match identically: $G_0 = 5.4, G_1 = 8.0, G_2 = 10.0, G_3 = 10.0$.

Problem 2.10.2 Level 1 • Direct Formula Drill OpenAI

QUESTION: An agent in state $s_t = A$ executes action $a_t = \text{Right}$. The current value in the Q-table is $Q(A, \text{Right}) = 3.50$. The agent observes reward $r_{t+1} = +2.0$ and transitions to state $s_{t+1} = B$.
The Q-table values for state $B$ are: $$Q(B, \text{Up}) = 4.0, \quad Q(B, \text{Down}) = 6.0, \quad Q(B, \text{Left}) = 1.5, \quad Q(B, \text{Right}) = 5.0$$ Given learning rate $\alpha = 0.20$ and discount factor $\gamma = 0.90$:

  1. Identify $\max_{a'} Q(B, a')$.
  2. Calculate the TD target $y_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma \max_{a'} Q(s_{t+1}, a')$.
  3. Calculate the TD error $\delta_t = y_t - Q(s_t, a_t)$.
  4. Compute the updated Q-value $Q_{\text{new}}(A, \text{Right})$.
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Mathematical Solution

Step 1: Find Maximum Q-Value in State B:
$$\max_{a'} Q(B, a') = \max(4.0, 6.0, 1.5, 5.0) = Q(B, \text{Down}) = 6.00$$

Step 2: Compute TD Target:
$$y_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma \max_{a'} Q(B, a') = 2.0 + (0.90 \times 6.00) = 2.0 + 5.40 = 7.40$$

Step 3: Compute TD Error:
$$\delta_t = y_t - Q(A, \text{Right}) = 7.40 - 3.50 = +3.90$$

Step 4: Update Q-Value:
$$Q_{\text{new}}(A, \text{Right}) = Q(A, \text{Right}) + \alpha \delta_t = 3.50 + (0.20 \times 3.90) = 3.50 + 0.78 = 4.28$$ The action-value increases from $3.50 \to 4.28$, reflecting the high expected future reward in state $B$.

Problem 2.10.3 Level 2 • Applied Engineering Trace Google

QUESTION: Consider a 3-state deterministic chain MDP with states $\mathcal{S} = \{s_1, s_2, s_3\}$ where $s_3$ is an absorbing terminal state. Two actions are available from non-terminal states $\mathcal{A} = \{\text{Stay}, \text{Move}\}$:

  • In $s_1$: $\text{Stay}$ yields $r = 0, s' = s_1$; $\text{Move}$ yields $r = 0, s' = s_2$.
  • In $s_2$: $\text{Stay}$ yields $r = 0, s' = s_2$; $\text{Move}$ yields $r = +10, s' = s_3$ (terminal).
  • In $s_3$: Absorbing, $V(s_3) = 0$.

With discount factor $\gamma = 0.50$ and initial value estimates $V_0(s_1) = 0, V_0(s_2) = 0, V_0(s_3) = 0$:

  1. Perform iteration 1 of Value Iteration to compute $V_1(s_1)$ and $V_1(s_2)$.
  2. Perform iteration 2 of Value Iteration to compute $V_2(s_1)$ and $V_2(s_2)$.
  3. Extract the greedy policy $\pi_2(s)$ derived from $V_2$.
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Algorithmic Trace

Step 1: Iteration 1 of Value Iteration:
Bellman optimality update: $V_{k+1}(s) = \max_a [R(s, a) + \gamma V_k(s')]$.

  • For state $s_1$: $$Q_1(s_1, \text{Stay}) = 0 + 0.5 V_0(s_1) = 0 + 0 = 0$$ $$Q_1(s_1, \text{Move}) = 0 + 0.5 V_0(s_2) = 0 + 0 = 0$$ $$V_1(s_1) = \max(0, 0) = 0.0$$
  • For state $s_2$: $$Q_1(s_2, \text{Stay}) = 0 + 0.5 V_0(s_2) = 0 + 0 = 0$$ $$Q_1(s_2, \text{Move}) = 10 + 0.5 V_0(s_3) = 10 + 0 = 10.0$$ $$V_1(s_2) = \max(0, 10.0) = 10.0$$
Value vector after iteration 1: $V_1 = [0.0, 10.0, 0.0]$.

Step 2: Iteration 2 of Value Iteration:
Using $V_1 = [0.0, 10.0, 0.0]$:

  • For state $s_1$: $$Q_2(s_1, \text{Stay}) = 0 + 0.5 V_1(s_1) = 0 + 0.5(0) = 0$$ $$Q_2(s_1, \text{Move}) = 0 + 0.5 V_1(s_2) = 0 + 0.5(10.0) = 5.0$$ $$V_2(s_1) = \max(0, 5.0) = 5.0$$
  • For state $s_2$: $$Q_2(s_2, \text{Stay}) = 0 + 0.5 V_1(s_2) = 0 + 0.5(10.0) = 5.0$$ $$Q_2(s_2, \text{Move}) = 10 + 0.5 V_1(s_3) = 10 + 0 = 10.0$$ $$V_2(s_2) = \max(5.0, 10.0) = 10.0$$
Value vector after iteration 2: $V_2 = [5.0, 10.0, 0.0]$.

Step 3: Greedy Policy Extraction:
$$\pi_2(s_1) = \arg\max_a Q_2(s_1, a) = \arg\max(0, 5.0) = \mathbf{\text{Move}}$$ $$\pi_2(s_2) = \arg\max_a Q_2(s_2, a) = \arg\max(5.0, 10.0) = \mathbf{\text{Move}}$$ In just 2 iterations, the information about the $+10$ goal reward has backpropagated across the chain to state $s_1$, yielding the optimal policy $\pi^* = \{\text{Move}, \text{Move}\}$.

Problem 2.10.4 Level 2 • Algorithmic Tracing Amazon

QUESTION: An agent is running reinforcement learning on a cliff-walking environment with $\gamma = 1.0$ and $\alpha = 0.5$. From current state $s$, the agent takes action $a$, receives reward $r = -1$, and lands in state $s'$.
In state $s'$, two actions are available: $$Q(s', \text{Safe}) = 0.0, \quad Q(s', \text{Cliff}) = -100.0$$ Under an $\epsilon$-greedy exploratory policy with $\epsilon = 0.3$, the agent rolls a random exploration action and picks $a' = \text{Cliff}$.
Given $Q(s, a) = -5.0$:

  1. Compute the updated value $Q_{\text{SARSA}}(s, a)$ under on-policy SARSA.
  2. Compute the updated value $Q_{\text{QLearning}}(s, a)$ under off-policy Q-Learning.
  3. Explain why SARSA learns a more cautious path than Q-Learning in dangerous environments.
✓ Complete Step-by-Step Algorithmic Comparison

Step 1: SARSA (On-Policy) Update:
SARSA updates using the actual action $a'$ executed by the behavior policy: $$\text{TD Target}_{\text{SARSA}} = r + \gamma Q(s', a') = -1 + 1.0 \times Q(s', \text{Cliff}) = -1 + (-100.0) = -101.0$$ $$Q_{\text{SARSA}}(s, a) = Q(s, a) + \alpha \left[ \text{TD Target}_{\text{SARSA}} - Q(s, a) \right]$$ $$Q_{\text{SARSA}}(s, a) = -5.0 + 0.5 \left[ -101.0 - (-5.0) \right] = -5.0 + 0.5(-96.0) = -5.0 - 48.0 = \mathbf{-53.0}$$

Step 2: Q-Learning (Off-Policy) Update:
Q-Learning updates using the maximum Q-value in $s'$, ignoring the exploration mistake $a'$: $$\text{TD Target}_{\text{QLearn}} = r + \gamma \max_{b} Q(s', b) = -1 + 1.0 \times \max(0.0, -100.0) = -1 + 0.0 = -1.0$$ $$Q_{\text{QLearn}}(s, a) = Q(s, a) + \alpha \left[ \text{TD Target}_{\text{QLearn}} - Q(s, a) \right]$$ $$Q_{\text{QLearn}}(s, a) = -5.0 + 0.5 \left[ -1.0 - (-5.0) \right] = -5.0 + 0.5(+4.0) = -5.0 + 2.0 = \mathbf{-3.0}$$

Step 3: Behavioral Implication:

  • Q-Learning assumes the agent will act completely greedily in the future ($\max_b Q$). It learns the mathematically shortest path right along the cliff edge, because that path is optimal under a deterministic policy. However, during online execution with $\epsilon > 0$, random exploration occasionally causes it to fall off the cliff!
  • SARSA accounts for the agent's exploratory nature ($\epsilon$-greedy). Because it penalizes actions that lead to states where exploration might trigger a catastrophic drop ($Q \to -53$), SARSA learns a wide, cautious path away from the cliff edge!

Problem 2.10.5 Level 3 • FAANG / Research Lab Proof Google DeepMind Meta

QUESTION: Define the Bellman Optimality Operator $\mathcal{T}$ acting on the space of bounded value functions $\mathcal{V} = \{V : \mathcal{S} \to \mathbb{R}\}$ equipped with the supremum norm $\|V\|_\infty = \max_{s \in \mathcal{S}} |V(s)|$ as: $$(\mathcal{T} V)(s) = \max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \left[ R(s, a) + \gamma \sum_{s' \in \mathcal{S}} P(s' \mid s, a) V(s') \right]$$ Prove that $\mathcal{T}$ is a $\gamma$-contraction mapping: $$\|\mathcal{T} V - \mathcal{T} U\|_\infty \le \gamma \|V - U\|_\infty$$ And invoke Banach's Fixed-Point Theorem to prove that Value Iteration converges uniquely to the optimal value function $V^*$.

✓ Complete Mathematical Proof of the Contraction Mapping Theorem

Step 1: Useful Algebraic Lemma on $\max$:
Let $f(a)$ and $g(a)$ be real-valued functions defined on a set $\mathcal{A}$.
For any $a \in \mathcal{A}$: $f(a) = f(a) - g(a) + g(a) \le g(a) + \max_{a'} |f(a') - g(a')|$.
Taking the maximum over $a$ on the left: $$\max_{a} f(a) \le \max_{a} g(a) + \max_{a} |f(a) - g(a)| \implies \max_a f(a) - \max_a g(a) \le \max_a |f(a) - g(a)|$$ By swapping $f$ and $g$, we obtain the reverse inequality: $$\left| \max_{a} f(a) - \max_{a} g(a) \right| \le \max_{a} |f(a) - g(a)|$$

Step 2: Apply the Lemma to $(\mathcal{T} V)(s) - (\mathcal{T} U)(s)$:
For an arbitrary state $s \in \mathcal{S}$: $$|(\mathcal{T} V)(s) - (\mathcal{T} U)(s)| = \left| \max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \left[ R(s, a) + \gamma \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) V(s') \right] - \max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \left[ R(s, a) + \gamma \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) U(s') \right] \right|$$ Applying the algebraic lemma from Step 1: $$\le \max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \left| \left( R(s, a) + \gamma \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) V(s') \right) - \left( R(s, a) + \gamma \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) U(s') \right) \right|$$ The immediate reward term $R(s, a)$ cancels out completely: $$= \max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \gamma \left| \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) (V(s') - U(s')) \right|$$

Step 3: Bound via the Supremum Norm:
By the triangle inequality and non-negativity of probabilities $P(s'|s, a) \ge 0$: $$\left| \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) (V(s') - U(s')) \right| \le \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) |V(s') - U(s')|$$ Since $|V(s') - U(s')| \le \max_{s''} |V(s'') - U(s'')| = \|V - U\|_\infty$: $$\le \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \|V - U\|_\infty = \|V - U\|_\infty \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a)$$ Since transition probabilities sum to $1$ ($\sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) = 1$): $$= \|V - U\|_\infty$$ Substituting this back: $$|(\mathcal{T} V)(s) - (\mathcal{T} U)(s)| \le \gamma \|V - U\|_\infty, \quad \forall s \in \mathcal{S}$$

Step 4: Take the Supremum Over All States $s$:
Taking the maximum over all $s \in \mathcal{S}$ on the left-hand side: $$\|\mathcal{T} V - \mathcal{T} U\|_\infty = \max_{s \in \mathcal{S}} |(\mathcal{T} V)(s) - (\mathcal{T} U)(s)| \le \gamma \|V - U\|_\infty$$ Since $\gamma \in [0, 1)$, $\mathcal{T}$ is a strict contraction mapping on the Banach space $(\mathcal{V}, \|\cdot\|_\infty)$.

Step 5: Invocation of Banach's Fixed-Point Theorem:
By Banach's Fixed-Point Theorem:

  1. There exists a unique fixed point $V^* \in \mathcal{V}$ such that $\mathcal{T} V^* = V^*$.
  2. For any arbitrary initial value function $V_0 \in \mathcal{V}$, the sequence generated by repeated application $V_{k+1} = \mathcal{T} V_k$ converges exponentially to $V^*$: $$\|V_k - V^*\|_\infty \le \frac{\gamma^k}{1 - \gamma} \|V_1 - V_0\|_\infty \to 0 \quad \text{as } k \to \infty$$
This guarantees that Value Iteration is mathematically guaranteed to converge to the exact optimal value function, with zero dependence on initialization! $\blacksquare$

Problem 2.10.6 Level 3 • FAANG / Research Lab Proof OpenAI Google DeepMind

QUESTION: Starting from the trajectory formulation $P(\tau; \theta) = \mu(s_0) \prod_{t=0}^T \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$ and expected return $J(\theta) = \int P(\tau; \theta) R(\tau) \, d\tau$, derive the Policy Gradient Theorem: $$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\tau \sim \pi_\theta} \left[ \sum_{t=0}^T \nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) \cdot G_t \right]$$ Explicitly show why the unknown environment transition dynamics $P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$ vanish from the gradient computation.

✓ Complete First-Principles Mathematical Derivation

Step 1: Expand the Objective Function:
The expected cumulative reward of trajectories $\tau = (s_0, a_0, s_1, a_1, \dots, s_T, a_T)$ is: $$J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\tau \sim \pi_\theta} [R(\tau)] = \int P(\tau; \theta) R(\tau) \, d\tau$$ Differentiating with respect to policy parameters $\theta$: $$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \nabla_\theta \int P(\tau; \theta) R(\tau) \, d\tau = \int \nabla_\theta P(\tau; \theta) R(\tau) \, d\tau$$

Step 2: The Log-Derivative (Likelihood-Ratio) Trick:
Notice that $\nabla_\theta \ln P(\tau; \theta) = \frac{\nabla_\theta P(\tau; \theta)}{P(\tau; \theta)} \implies \nabla_\theta P(\tau; \theta) = P(\tau; \theta) \nabla_\theta \ln P(\tau; \theta)$.
Substituting this into the integral: $$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \int P(\tau; \theta) \nabla_\theta \ln P(\tau; \theta) R(\tau) \, d\tau = \mathbb{E}_{\tau \sim \pi_\theta} [\nabla_\theta \ln P(\tau; \theta) R(\tau)]$$

Step 3: Expand the Trajectory Log-Probability:
The probability of trajectory $\tau$ is: $$P(\tau; \theta) = \mu(s_0) \prod_{t=0}^T \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$$ Taking the natural logarithm: $$\ln P(\tau; \theta) = \ln \mu(s_0) + \sum_{t=0}^T \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) + \sum_{t=0}^T \ln P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$$ Now take the gradient with respect to $\theta$:

  • The initial state distribution $\mu(s_0)$ is determined by the environment, so $\nabla_\theta \ln \mu(s_0) = \mathbf{0}$.
  • The transition dynamics $P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$ depend only on physics/environment rules, completely independent of policy parameters $\theta$, so $\nabla_\theta \ln P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t) = \mathbf{0}$!
Therefore: $$\nabla_\theta \ln P(\tau; \theta) = \sum_{t=0}^T \nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t)$$ The unknown environment dynamics vanish completely!

Step 4: Causality and Return-to-Go $G_t$:
Substituting back: $$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\tau} \left[ \sum_{t=0}^T \nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) \left( \sum_{t'=0}^T r_{t'+1} \right) \right]$$ By the law of causality, actions taken at time $t$ cannot affect rewards received in the past ($t' < t$). Formally, for all $t' < t$: $$\mathbb{E}_{\tau} [\nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) r_{t'+1}] = \mathbf{0}$$ Removing past rewards leaves only future returns from step $t$ onward (the return-to-go $G_t = \sum_{t'=t}^T \gamma^{t'-t} r_{t'+1}$): $$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\tau \sim \pi_\theta} \left[ \sum_{t=0}^T \nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) \cdot G_t \right]$$ This completes the derivation of the REINFORCE Policy Gradient. $\blacksquare$

Problem 2.10.7 Level 4 • Conceptual Trap MCQ Google DeepMind

QUESTION: In reinforcement learning theory (Sutton & Barto), the Deadly Triad refers to the combination of three algorithmic properties that, when present simultaneously, can cause value function estimates to diverge uncontrollably. What are the three components of the Deadly Triad?

  1. Stochastic Policies, Continuous Actions, and Sparse Rewards.
  2. Function Approximation, Bootstrapping, and Off-Policy Learning.
  3. High Learning Rate, Low Discount Factor, and Deep Replay Buffers.
  4. Markov Property, Stationary Transitions, and Greedy Action Selection.
✓ Correct Answer: B • Comprehensive Distractor Analysis

Detailed Analysis:
The Deadly Triad consists of:

  1. Function Approximation: Parameterizing value functions using scalable function approximators (deep neural networks, linear basis expansion) rather than tabular lookup.
  2. Bootstrapping: Updating value targets using existing estimated value predictions (TD learning: $r + \gamma V(s')$ or $r + \gamma \max Q(s', a')$) rather than complete Monte Carlo rollouts ($G_t$).
  3. Off-Policy Learning: Learning about a target policy (such as greedy $\pi^*$) from transitions generated by a different behavior policy (such as $\epsilon$-greedy exploration).
When all three are present (as in standard naive Q-learning with deep networks), the Bellman contraction mapping property is broken. Errors in overestimating Q-values get bootstrapped into target labels, function approximation generalizes the error across the entire state space, and off-policy distribution mismatch compounds the divergence, causing Q-values to explode to infinity.
DQN stabilizes the deadly triad through Experience Replay (correcting off-policy distribution shifts) and Target Networks (freezing bootstrapping targets).

Why Other Options are False:

  • A is FALSE: Continuous actions and sparse rewards create optimization and exploration difficulty, but do not mathematically cause value divergence.
  • C is FALSE: Replay buffers stabilize training; they are the solution to the triad, not part of the problem.
  • D is FALSE: The Markov property and stationary transitions are the very axioms that make RL theoretically tractable.

Problem 2.10.8 Level 4 • Assertion-Reasoning & PPO Clipping Trap OpenAI

QUESTION: Evaluate the following Assertion and Reason regarding Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO):

Assertion (A): In the PPO clipped objective $\mathcal{L}^{\text{CLIP}}(\theta) = \hat{\mathbb{E}}_t \left[ \min\left( r_t(\theta) \hat{A}_t, \; \operatorname{clip}(r_t(\theta), 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon) \hat{A}_t \right) \right]$, when the advantage is negative ($\hat{A}_t < 0$, indicating an action worse than average), the objective places a strict lower bound on how much the policy can reduce the action's probability, preventing catastrophic gradient collapse.

Reason (R): When $\hat{A}_t < 0$, multiplying the clipped interval $[1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon]$ by a negative number inverts the order of terms: if the new policy makes the action much less likely ($r_t(\theta) < 1 - \epsilon$), the clipped product $(1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t$ is mathematically larger than the unclipped product $r_t(\theta)\hat{A}_t$, causing the $\min$ operator to select the unclipped term and allow gradients to pass freely.

  1. Both (A) and (R) are true, and (R) is the correct mathematical explanation of (A).
  2. Both (A) and (R) are true, but (R) is NOT the correct explanation of (A).
  3. (A) is true, but (R) is false.
  4. (A) is false, but (R) is true.
✓ Correct Answer: C • Comprehensive Mathematical Dissection

Detailed Analysis:
Let us carefully evaluate the mathematics of the PPO objective when $\hat{A}_t < 0$ (a bad action):
We want the policy to reduce the probability of this action, meaning $\pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t) < \pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(a_t \mid s_t) \implies r_t(\theta) < 1.0$.
The objective is: $$\mathcal{L}(r_t) = \min\left( r_t \hat{A}_t, \; \operatorname{clip}(r_t, 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon) \hat{A}_t \right)$$ Let $\hat{A}_t = -|A|$. Then: $$\mathcal{L}(r_t) = \min\left( -r_t |A|, \; -\operatorname{clip}(r_t, 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon) |A| \right) = - \max\left( r_t |A|, \; \operatorname{clip}(r_t, 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon) |A| \right)$$

Case Analysis for $\hat{A}_t < 0$:

  • Case 1: $1 - \epsilon \le r_t \le 1 + \epsilon$: Here $\operatorname{clip}(r_t) = r_t$. Both terms are identical, and gradient $\nabla_\theta r_t \hat{A}_t$ updates the policy normally.
  • Case 2: The policy decreases probability too drastically ($r_t < 1 - \epsilon$):
    Here $\operatorname{clip}(r_t, 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon) = 1 - \epsilon$.
    Comparing the two terms: Since $r_t < 1 - \epsilon$, multiplying by negative $\hat{A}_t$ gives: $$r_t \hat{A}_t > (1 - \epsilon) \hat{A}_t$$ The $\min$ operator selects the smaller value: $$\min(r_t \hat{A}_t, (1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t) = (1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t$$ Notice that $(1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t$ is a constant with respect to $\theta$! Its gradient $\nabla_\theta [(1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t] = \mathbf{0}$!

Conclusion on Assertion and Reason:

  • Assertion (A) is TRUE: When $r_t < 1 - \epsilon$, the objective becomes constant and the gradient drops to zero. This halts further reduction of the action's probability, preventing the policy from over-correcting and driving probabilities to absolute zero.
  • Reason (R) is FALSE: Reason (R) claims that when $r_t < 1 - \epsilon$, the $\min$ selects the unclipped term $r_t \hat{A}_t$. As shown above, because $(1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t$ is more negative, the $\min$ selects the clipped term $(1 - \epsilon)\hat{A}_t$, thereby zeroing out the gradient. Reason (R) has the inequality backwards!
Therefore, (A) is true, but (R) is false. $\blacksquare$

8. Production Code Lab 2.10: Tabular Q-Learning from Scratch

Below is a complete, educational implementation of tabular Q-Learning with $\epsilon$-greedy exploration, solving a grid-world navigation problem.

Code Lab 2.10 — Q-Learning Agent from First Principles

🔗 View in GitHub: part2_classical_ml/ch210_reinforcement_learning/q_learning_scratch.py

import numpy as np
from collections import defaultdict

class GridWorld:
    """Simple 4x4 Grid World MDP with goal and trap states."""
    def __init__(self, size=4):
        self.size = size
        self.goal = (size - 1, size - 1)
        self.traps = [(1, 1), (2, 3)]
        self.state = (0, 0)

    def reset(self):
        self.state = (0, 0)
        return self.state

    def step(self, action):
        """Actions: 0=Up, 1=Down, 2=Left, 3=Right"""
        r, c = self.state
        if action == 0: r = max(r - 1, 0)
        elif action == 1: r = min(r + 1, self.size - 1)
        elif action == 2: c = max(c - 1, 0)
        elif action == 3: c = min(c + 1, self.size - 1)

        self.state = (r, c)

        if self.state == self.goal:
            return self.state, +10.0, True    # Goal reward
        elif self.state in self.traps:
            return self.state, -5.0, True     # Trap penalty
        else:
            return self.state, -0.1, False    # Step cost

class QLearningAgent:
    def __init__(self, n_actions=4, alpha=0.1, gamma=0.95, epsilon=1.0):
        self.q_table = defaultdict(lambda: np.zeros(n_actions))
        self.alpha = alpha
        self.gamma = gamma
        self.epsilon = epsilon
        self.n_actions = n_actions

    def choose_action(self, state):
        """Epsilon-greedy action selection"""
        if np.random.random() < self.epsilon:
            return np.random.randint(self.n_actions)
        return int(np.argmax(self.q_table[state]))

    def learn(self, state, action, reward, next_state, done):
        """Q-Learning TD update: Q(s, a) <- Q(s, a) + alpha * [r + gamma * max Q(s', a') - Q(s, a)]"""
        td_target = reward + (0.0 if done else self.gamma * np.max(self.q_table[next_state]))
        td_error = td_target - self.q_table[state][action]
        self.q_table[state][action] += self.alpha * td_error
        return td_error

if __name__ == "__main__":
    env = GridWorld(size=4)
    agent = QLearningAgent(n_actions=4, alpha=0.1, gamma=0.95, epsilon=1.0)

    n_episodes = 2000
    rewards_per_episode = []

    for episode in range(n_episodes):
        state = env.reset()
        total_reward = 0

        for step in range(100):
            action = agent.choose_action(state)
            next_state, reward, done = env.step(action)
            agent.learn(state, action, reward, next_state, done)
            state = next_state
            total_reward += reward
            if done:
                break

        rewards_per_episode.append(total_reward)
        agent.epsilon = max(0.01, agent.epsilon * 0.998)

    last_100 = rewards_per_episode[-100:]
    print(f"Average Reward (last 100 episodes): {np.mean(last_100):.2f}")
    print(f"Success Rate (last 100 episodes)  : {sum(1 for r in last_100 if r > 0) / 100:.0%}")
    print(f"\nLearned Q-Values for start state (0,0):")
    actions = ["Up", "Down", "Left", "Right"]
    for i, a in enumerate(actions):
        print(f"  {a:6s}: Q = {agent.q_table[(0,0)][i]:.4f}")
Chapter 2.10 Dedicated Project & Case Study

Project 2.10: Deep Q-Network Atari Game Agent with Experience Replay

Architect and train a complete DQN agent with convolutional state encoder, experience replay buffer, target network synchronization, and epsilon-greedy exploration to play Atari games (CartPole, LunarLander) from raw pixel observations in PyTorch.

🔗 Full Project Code: part2_classical_ml/ch210_reinforcement_learning/q_learning_scratch.py

Case Study Architecture: In 2013, DeepMind demonstrated that a single DQN architecture could achieve superhuman performance on 49 different Atari 2600 games, learning directly from raw screen pixels with no game-specific engineering. In this project, you construct the complete DQN pipeline: preprocessing raw frames (grayscale, resize, frame stacking), building a 3-layer CNN state encoder, implementing a circular experience replay buffer with prioritized sampling, synchronized target network updates every 10,000 steps, and training to convergence on CartPole-v1 and LunarLander-v2. You visualize training curves (reward vs. episodes), Q-value distributions, and epsilon decay schedules.
